# Sūrah Al-Baqarah (Chapter 2) — Verse-by-Verse Tafsir

## Introduction to the Sūrah

Al-Baqarah is the longest sūrah in the Qur'an. It holds 286 verses, and it stands at the head of the Madinan period, when the community had a city, a treasury, neighbours of other faiths, and a state to run. Al-Qurṭubī records that the sūrah came down in Madinah in scattered instalments, and that the scholars counted it the first sūrah revealed there. A few of its verses belong to a much later moment. (2:281 — *“then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged”*) came down at Minā on the Day of Sacrifice during the Farewell Pilgrimage, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the verses on interest were also among the last parts of the Qur'an to arrive.
The sūrah takes its name from a story inside it. Moses told his people that God was commanding them to slaughter a cow — (2:67 — *“Allah commands you to sacrifice a cow.”*) — and they answered with so many questions that the command tightened around one particular animal. The whole exchange sits in this sūrah, and the chapter is named after it. A book named after a cow is a book at home in ordinary life: fields, debts, nursing mothers, marriage, and the animals a household lives on.
The early scholars weighed its place in the Book. Khālid ibn Maʿdān called it the pavilion of the Qur'an, al-Qurṭubī reports, and the name suits a sūrah of this size, carrying this much law and this much warning.
Its virtues are recorded in the collections, and Ibn Kathīr gathers them. Aḥmad, Muslim, At-Tirmidhī and An-Nasāʾī record Abū Hurayrah reporting the Prophet's ﷺ words: do not turn your houses into graves, for the house in which Sūrah al-Baqarah is recited is not entered by Shayṭān. At-Tirmidhī graded the report ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ. An-Nasāʾī records Ibn Masʿūd saying that Shayṭān leaves the house in which al-Baqarah is heard, and al-Ḥākim entered that report in al-Mustadrak and judged its chain authentic. Ad-Dārimī records Ibn Masʿūd's advice that ten verses recited at night keep Shayṭān from entering the house until morning: the first four verses, Āyat al-Kursī, the two verses after it, and the last three. Ibn Kathīr also carries a report of Sahl ibn Saʿd, recorded by al-Ṭabarānī, Ibn Ḥibbān and Ibn Mardawayh, that everything has a hump and the hump of the Qur'an is al-Baqarah; whoever recites it in his house at night keeps Shayṭān away for three nights, and in the day for three days.
Two passages inside it stand out further. Ibn Kathīr notes that the authentic reports call the Verse of the Seat the greatest verse in the Book of Allah, and he records from Aḥmad the exchange in which the Prophet ﷺ asked ʿUbayy ibn Kaʿb which verse of Allah's Book was greatest. ʿUbayy named (2:255 — *“Allah! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him, the Ever-Living, All-Sustaining.”*), and the Prophet ﷺ said, may the knowledge delight you, Abū al-Mundhir. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān records the report about the sūrah's closing lines, that whoever recites them at night has them as a sufficiency for him.
The sūrah opens with three kinds of people walking three different roads. Verses 1 to 5 describe the believers. Verses 6 and 7 describe those who reject the message outright, and verses 8 to 20 describe the hypocrites, who say one thing and mean another. Mujāhid counted them exactly that way — four verses, two verses, thirteen verses — and Ibn Kathīr records his statement. From there the sūrah turns to humanity at large, then to the Children of Israel and the long account of their covenant, and then to the laws that shaped a community: fasting, pilgrimage, marriage, trade, loans, interest, and the rules of war. It closes with the prayer of the believers and the answer to it.
One thread ties this sūrah to the one before it. Al-Fātiḥah ends with a request: (1:6 — *“Guide us along the Straight Path”*). Al-Baqarah opens by putting down a Book and calling it a guide. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the two as question and answer — a community asking to be shown the road, and a Lord answering with the Book that shows it.

---

## Verse 2:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm

**“Alif-Lãm-Mĩm”**

These three letters belong to a family the scholars call al-muqaṭṭaʿāt, the cut letters. They are pronounced one by one, each with its own name, and nothing joins them into a word. Twenty-nine sūrahs open this way, as Abu Bakr al-Jazāʾirī counts them: this sūrah first, and Sūrah al-Qalam, which opens with Nūn, last. Some openings are a single letter, such as Ṣād and Qāf. Some are two, such as Ṭā-Hā and Yā-Sīn. Some run to three, four or five, such as Alif-Lãm-Mĩm-Ṣād and Kāf-Hā-Yā-ʿAyn-Ṣād.

No explanation of these letters reaches us from the Prophet ﷺ, and al-Jazāʾirī says plainly that nothing on their meaning is established from him. Al-Jalālayn settles the matter in a few words: God knows best what He means by these letters. Al-Saʿdī takes the same road and gives the rule behind it. The safest thing is silence about a meaning we have no revealed basis for, joined to a firm belief that God did not send these letters as play. They carry a wisdom we have not been handed, and the Muslim rests there.

The early scholars still gathered the possibilities, and al-Ṭabarī sets them down with their chains. Qatādah said the letters are a name of the Qur'an, and Mujāhid and Ibn Jurayj are recorded saying the same. Others called them openings with which God opens His Book. Ibn Kathīr reports that Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, ʿAlī and Ibn Masʿūd held that their knowledge stays with God, and that some scholars said they are the names of the sūrahs they begin. One further view comes from the linguists, and al-Ṭabarī reports it as well: the letters are simply the letters of the alphabet, named one by one, the way a father says that his son recites alif, bā, tā — meaning the whole alphabet even though he stops early. Al-Kashshāf adds the small point that fixes the recitation. These are names, like the names of numbers, so they are said by their names; a governing word in front of them can carry them into a case, and otherwise they stand still.

Two sayings from the first generation appear in nearly every book on the subject. Abū Bakr is reported to have said that every book has a mystery, and that the mystery of the Qur'an lies in the openings of its sūrahs. ʿAlī is reported to have said that every book has a quintessence, and that the quintessence of this Book is the letters of spelling. Al-Baghawī records both. Ibn Kathīr counts the letters without repetition and reaches fourteen: alif, lām, mīm, ṣād, rā, kāf, hā, yā, ʿayn, ṭā, sīn, ḥā, qāf, nūn. And the scholars agree on one thing — God did not reveal them as a game. Anyone who says that part of the Qur'an means nothing has made a heavy mistake, and the verse that answers such talk is (3:7 — *“We believe in this ˹Quran˺—it is all from our Lord.”*)

The purpose the later scholars settled on is the one al-Mukhtaṣar states in a single line: the letters point to the miracle of a Book built from the same letters people already speak with, which is why an opening like this is usually followed by a verse about the Qur'an — and here it is 2:2. Ibn Kathīr traces that view through the books. Al-Rāzī related it to al-Mubarrid, al-Qurṭubī related it to al-Farrāʾ and Quṭrub, and al-Zamakhsharī adopted it. Al-Zamakhsharī explains why the challenge arrives in pieces of one, two, three, four and five letters, and his reason is simple: human speech is built from such pieces, so the challenge was repeated in every size the doubters used. Abu Bakr al-Jazāʾirī adds a second benefit. The leaders of Makkah were working to stop the Qur'an from being heard, and this odd opening caught their ears; once they listened, the words reached them. Al-Baghawī adds the plainest benefit of the three: the letters ask one thing from us, and that is to believe them and recite them exactly as they came down.

Picture a builder who has finished a house that has the whole town talking. On the morning of the argument he tips a bag onto the table: a pile of ordinary clay bricks, the kind anyone can dig from the riverbank. Then he says, you have these too. Build it. That is the shape of this opening. The alphabet is the bricks, and the claim is the building.

---

## Verse 2:2

> This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**“This is the Book!”**

The Arabic opens with a pointer to something far off: that Book, not this Book. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notices the oddity at once. The Book is right in front of the listener, so why push it away with the far pointer? Al-Ṭabarī records that most of the exegetes read the two words as carrying one sense, and he defends them with a habit of Arabic speech. Once words have been spoken, they belong to the past, and Arabic will point at something just mentioned the way it points at something absent: a man hears his friend describe an event and answers, that is exactly what you said. The talk about the letters has only just ended, so the Book arrives as what was just named.

Al-Ṭabarī offers a second reading of the same words: that Book may mean the sūrahs revealed before this one, since they belong to the one Book. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān gives the reading that makes the whole chapter click. The pointer reaches past the letters, back to the end of al-Fātiḥah, to (1:6 — *“Guide us along the Straight Path”*). The Muslim asked for the road, and that Book, the one asked for, is here.

**“There is no doubt about it”**

The line denies doubt about the Book, and the earliest generations were of one voice on its sense. Ibn Kathīr gathers the names: Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd, Abū al-Dardāʾ, Mujāhid, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, ʿAṭāʾ, Qatādah and others, all reading it as a statement that the Book is from God without doubt. Ibn Abī Ḥātim adds that he knows of no disagreement on it. Al-Mukhtaṣar sharpens the point: there is nothing of doubt in it, neither in where it came from nor in what it says.

Al-Saʿdī draws a rule out of the wording that is worth keeping. When the Qur'an denies something in order to praise, the denial carries its opposite along with it. A bare absence is not praise. A house free of sickness is not praised unless it is full of health. So the denial of doubt here carries certainty: the Book holds the clear knowledge that removes doubt. Certainty is not a side matter either, because guidance does not arrive without it, which is why the next words speak of guidance.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sorts doubt into two kinds. The claim itself may be false, or the listener may have misread it. The Book answers the first kind with an invitation to test it: (2:23 — *“And if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant, then produce a sûrah like it and call your helpers other than Allah, if what you say is true”*). The second kind is answered by teaching, because a person who doubts out of confusion needs the explanation this sūrah spends its length supplying. The same claim appears elsewhere in shorter form: (32:2 — *“The revelation of this Book is—beyond doubt—from the Lord of all worlds”*).

**“a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺”**

The word here is hudā, guidance, and al-Ṭabarī takes it from the root: to show a man the road, point him to it and make it clear. He records al-Shaʿbī's gloss, guidance out of error, and the report of Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd that the Book is light for the mindful. Then he faces the question the wording raises. If the Book is guidance, why is it guidance for the mindful alone? His answer is worth reading twice. The Book is light for the mindful, a healing for the hearts of believers, a heaviness in the ears of those who deny it, blindness for those who refuse it, and a binding proof against those who reject it. One book, and different results, because the state of the reader is not the same.

Ibn Kathīr records how the early community defined taqwā. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb asked Ubayy ibn Kaʿb what taqwā was, and Ubayy asked him whether he had ever walked a path lined with thorns. ʿUmar said he had. Ubayy asked what he did then. ʿUmar said he rolled up his sleeves and picked his way with care. Ubayy said, that is taqwā. Ibn Kathīr calls the root of the word the act of guarding yourself from what you dislike, and he supports the picture from the Qur'an: (90:10 — *“and shown them the two ways ˹of right and wrong˺”*).

Think of a lamp in a room. It does not choose who sees by it; it lights the room the same way for everyone. What decides whether a man finds the door is whether his eyes are open and whether he turned toward it. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān counts three levels of guidance. Every creature receives the first level simply by being made and directed to what it needs. The second is offered to people who can choose, and the third is given to those who act on what they are shown. This verse promises the last two to the person who guards himself.

---

## Verse 2:3

> who believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and donate from what We have provided for them

**“who believe in the unseen”**

Al-Ṭabarī fills this phrase with reports, and his witnesses do not give one definition. Ibn ʿAbbās said the unseen is what comes from God, meaning the revelation itself. A report carried from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd, along with other Companions, names Paradise, the Fire, and everything God mentioned in the Qur'an, and adds a sharp observation: the believers among the Arabs did not take this belief from a book they already owned, or from knowledge they had inherited. Zirr ibn Ḥubaysh, a Successor, said the unseen is the Qur'an. Qatādah listed Paradise, the Fire, the raising of the dead and the Day of Judgement. Al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas included God, His angels, His messengers, the Last Day with its garden and its fire, the meeting with Him, and life after death. Al-Ṭabarī then gives the root meaning that holds all of these together: the unseen is every thing hidden from you.

The word īmān steps in here, and the earliest scholars explained it. Al-Ṭabarī reports that Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd glossed the verb as taṣdīq — confirming, accepting as true — and al-Qurṭubī finds the same sense in the language, with the Qur'an's own use of it on the lips of Joseph's brothers: (12:17 — *“But you will not believe us, no matter how truthful we are.”*). Al-Ṭabarī's own settled view, which Ibn Kathīr reports at length, is that faith in the unseen is belief with the heart, speech with the tongue and work with the limbs, and that the word, used without restriction, holds all three.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out why this phrase sits at the head of the list. If a man tells you the cloth in front of you is black and you agree, that is confirmation, not faith, because you agreed on what your own eyes handed you. Faith begins where the eyes stop. It is accepting a report with full trust in the one who gave it, without demanding to see it first. That is why the first thing the Book says about the people it guides is not how often they pray or how much they give. It is that they trust the unseen.

**“establish prayer”**

The verb is not do the prayer; it is establish it, and the early scholars explained the difference. Ibn ʿAbbās said it means to perform the prayer with everything that goes with it. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk, reporting from him, said it means to complete the bowing, the prostration, the recitation, the humility and the attending. Qatādah said it means to guard the times, the washing, the bowing and the prostration. Muqātil ibn Ḥayyān said it means to keep the time, finish the purity the prayer needs, and complete its bowing, prostration, recitation, witness and blessings. A body standing in prayer while the mind wanders through the day is doing something; establishing the prayer is another matter entirely.

**“and donate from what We have provided for them”**

The phrase begins with from, and the preposition carries weight: the Muslim gives out of what he holds, not the whole of it. The early commentators differed about which giving was meant first. Ibn ʿAbbās called it the obligatory alms on wealth. A report from Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and other Companions says it was a man's spending on his own family, before the law of alms came down. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk, in a report Ibn Kathīr carries, said it meant giving freely according to what a person could manage, until the rules of charity arrived later in the Qur'an. Ibn Kathīr notices how often the Book joins prayer and giving in one breath, and gives the reason: prayer is the right of God, and giving is the right of His creatures, so the two together cover a person's duty in both directions.

The three qualities climb in a deliberate order, from the inward to the outward. Belief in the unseen is hidden inside a man, where no neighbour can see it. Prayer is half hidden and half visible: a body stands and bows, and only God knows what the heart brought. Giving leaves the pocket and enters the street, where the poor can see it and the ledger can record it. Think of a sailor steering by a star he cannot touch. He is not being foolish; the star has never failed anyone who steered by it. The believer steers his life by things he cannot see, and the two qualities that follow make that trust visible: a body that stands in prayer, and a hand that gives what it holds.

---

## Verse 2:4

> and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ and what was revealed before you, and have sure faith in the Hereafter

**“and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺”**

Ibn ʿAbbās gives the sense in a sentence: they believe in what Allah sent you with, and in what the earlier messengers were sent with; they do not split one messenger from another, and they reject nothing that came from their Lord. Al-Mukhtaṣar says the same in fewer words: without distinction. A believer does not pick a favourite prophet and discard the rest, because the messages come from one sender and the messengers spoke for one Lord.

**“and what was revealed before you”**

This line raises a question the commentators took seriously. Does the description continue the believers of the previous verse, or does it introduce a second group? Al-Ṭabarī carries a report from al-Suddī that the two groups are the believers among the Arabs and the believers among the People of the Book, who had followed the earlier scriptures and then accepted the Prophet ﷺ, and that the last verse of the passage gathers both. Read either way, the lesson stands. Ibn Kathīr takes the wider reading and explains why the qualities belong together. Nobody believes in the unseen, prays and gives while rejecting the messengers, because these traits lean on each other; a person who has them cannot tear the message away from the Messenger who brought it. He cites the command that keeps believers steady in exactly this position: (4:136 — *“O believers! Have faith in Allah, His Messenger, the Book He has revealed to His Messenger, and the Scriptures He revealed before.”*). The Qur'an also holds out the People of the Book who did believe as an honoured example: (3:199 — *“Indeed, there are some among the People of the Book who truly believe in Allah and what has been revealed to you ˹believers˺ and what was revealed to them.”*).

Al-Ṭabarī carries a second report through the same chain that narrows the description: these are the believers among the People of the Book. He then leaves the two readings standing side by side, and the grammar permits both, since the words can hang from the previous description or open a fresh one. Either way the Book honours one thing: a person who accepts every messenger God sent, in the order God sent them. The final scripture does not cancel what came before it. It confirms it, and the Qur'an says as much about its own coming: (5:48 — *“We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ this Book with the truth, as a confirmation of previous Scriptures and a supreme authority on them.”*). The believers are taught to put the same thing into words: (2:285 — *“We make no distinction between any of His messengers.”*).

**“and have sure faith in the Hereafter”**

Ibn Kathīr spells out what certainty covers here: the raising of the dead, the standing for the account, Paradise, the Fire, the reckoning, and the scale that weighs deeds. He also explains why that world is called the Hereafter: because it comes after this one. Al-Mukhtaṣar sums it up as belief in the Afterlife with its rewards and punishments.

Why single this out after the other qualities? Because the other three lean on it. A man who expects no account at the end will not keep praying when prayer costs him his sleep, and he will not keep giving when giving takes from his own pocket. Certainty about the world to come is the beam that holds the other qualities up. The word itself, yūqinūn, is stronger than thinking or hoping; it names a knowledge that has settled and left no room for wobble.

Picture an investor who buys land along a road that has not been built yet. His friends think he is throwing money away; he thinks he is early. What a man does with his money follows what he believes about the future, and everyone behaves this way with the things he is sure of. The verse says the believer is sure of the world that is coming, and that certainty shows up in his hands: in how he prays, and in what he gives.

Ibn Kathīr draws the practical line out of all this. A man who accepts the parts of revelation that suit him and drops the rest has not believed in revelation; he has believed in himself with a scripture in his hand. A phrase like what was revealed before you closes that door, because it asks a believer to honour a message he never heard, delivered by a messenger he never met, on the strength of the One who sent it.

---

## Verse 2:5

> It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord, and it is they who will be successful

**“It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord”**

The passage closes with a pointer: those, not these. Al-Kashshāf and al-Bayḍāwī explain the work that pointer does. When the Book said earlier that it is guidance for the mindful, a listener could ask why the mindful were singled out for it. The three qualities that follow — trusting the unseen, prayer, giving — are the answer, and the pointer gathers them up: those people, with those qualities, are the ones guidance belongs to. Al-Bayḍāwī adds that the pointer is stronger than repeating the noun, because it throws the weight on the description and shows that the ruling follows from it.

Then comes the phrase that carries the promise: upon guidance from their Lord. Al-Bayḍāwī reads the word upon as a picture, the way a rider is upon an animal. Guidance is not something these people visit now and then; it is something they are settled on and carried by. And it comes from their Lord, not from their own cleverness. Ibn Kathīr calls it light, proof and insight, and al-Mukhtaṣar calls it the road that leads to Him.

Al-Ṭabarī asks who the pointer covers, and his answer is generous. He reports that it gathers both groups described in the two earlier verses — the believers among the Arabs, and the believers among the People of the Book — and that the promise of guidance and success belongs to them together. Al-Ṭabarī also records two narrower readings: that the pointer covers only the mindful, or only the believers among the People of the Book. Then he returns to the grammar and shows why the argument can go either way. The words "and those who believe" can hang from the end of the first description or open a new sentence, and the closing verse settles the dispute by gathering everyone the passage has described into one promise. Difference of background does not divide the people of this Book, and the point is worth holding on to in a sūrah that will soon speak hard words to the Children of Israel.

**“and it is they who will be successful”**

Al-Jalālayn spells success out plainly: they will enter Paradise and be saved from the Fire. Ibn Kathīr widens it to both lives, saying they gain what they were seeking and are kept from what they were dreading, in this world and the next. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps both halves: they gain what they desire, and they are saved from what they fear.

The Arabic word carries a picture that English leaves behind. Falāḥ means thriving and success, and the older language ties it to cultivation, to soil breaking open and a plant coming through. The call to prayer still uses the word, since after the call to prayer comes the call to falāḥ. So the sūrah's first portrait of a believer is a farmer's picture. He plants what no eye can see under the ground, and that is trust in the unseen. He waters it on time, and that is the prayer. He shares the crop, and that is the giving. The harvest is what the verse names. The five verses just read are the measuring line of the sūrah, and the verses that follow show the people who fail it: those who reject the message outright, and those who talk like believers while living as strangers to the Book.

Success in this sūrah is never a private mood or a feeling in the chest. It is a promise with a picture attached: a road that a person is settled on, a Lord who gave it, and a harvest waiting at the end of the season.

## Verse 2:187

> It has been made permissible for you to be intimate with your wives during the nights preceding the fast. Your spouses are a garment for you as you are for them. Allah knows that you were deceiving yourselves. So He has accepted your repentance and pardoned you. So now you may be intimate with them and seek what Allah has prescribed for you. ˹You may˺ eat and drink until you see the light of dawn breaking the darkness of night, then complete the fast until nightfall. Do not be intimate with your spouses while you are meditating in the mosques. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not exceed them. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to people, so they may become mindful ˹of Him˺

## Verse 2:196

> Complete the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage for Allah. But if prevented ˹from proceeding˺, then ˹offer˺ whatever sacrificial animals you can afford. And do not shave your heads until the sacrificial animal reaches its destination. But if any of you is ill or has a scalp ailment ˹requiring shaving˺, then compensate either by fasting, charity, or a sacrificial offering. In times of peace, you may combine the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage then make the sacrificial offering you can afford. Whoever cannot afford that ˹offering˺, let them fast three days during pilgrimage and seven after returning ˹home˺—completing ten. These offerings are for those who do not live near the Sacred House. And be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is severe in punishment

## Verse 2:217

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about fighting in the sacred months. Say, “Fighting during these months is a great sin. But hindering ˹others˺ from the Path of Allah, rejecting Him, and expelling the worshippers from the Sacred Mosque is ˹a˺ greater ˹sin˺ in the sight of Allah. For persecution is far worse than killing. And they will not stop fighting you until they turn you away from your faith—if they can. And whoever among you renounces their own faith and dies a disbeliever, their deeds will become void in this life and in the Hereafter. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

## Verse 2:219

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about intoxicants and gambling. Say, “There is great evil in both, as well as some benefit for people—but the evil outweighs the benefit.” They ˹also˺ ask you ˹O Prophet˺ what they should donate. ## Verse 2:229

> Divorce may be retracted twice, then the husband must retain ˹his wife˺ with honour or separate ˹from her˺ with grace. It is not lawful for husbands to take back anything of the dowry given to their wives, unless the couple fears not being able to keep within the limits of Allah. So if you fear they will not be able to keep within the limits of Allah, there is no blame if the wife compensates the husband to obtain divorce. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not transgress them. ## Verse 2:233

> ˹Divorced˺ mothers will breastfeed their offspring for two whole years, for those who wish to complete the nursing ˹of their child˺. The child’s father will provide reasonable maintenance and clothing for the mother ˹during that period˺. No one will be charged with more than they can bear. No mother or father should be made to suffer for their child. The ˹father’s˺ heirs are under the same obligation. But if both sides decide—after mutual consultation and consent—to wean a child, then there is no blame on them. If you decide to have your children nursed by a wet-nurse, it is permissible as long as you pay fairly. Be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

## Verse 2:249

> When Saul marched forth with his army, he cautioned: “Allah will test you with a river. So whoever drinks ˹his fill˺ from it is not with me, and whoever does not taste it—except a sip from the hollow of his hands—is definitely with me.” They all drank ˹their fill˺ except for a few! When he and the ˹remaining˺ faithful with him crossed the river, they said, “Now we are no match for Goliath and his warriors.” But those ˹believers˺ who were certain they would meet Allah reasoned, “How many times has a small force vanquished a mighty army by the Will of Allah! And Allah is ˹always˺ with the steadfast.”

## Verse 2:259

> Or ˹are you not aware of˺ the one who passed by a city which was in ruins. He wondered, “How could Allah bring this back to life after its destruction?” So Allah caused him to die for a hundred years then brought him back to life. Allah asked, “How long have you remained ˹in this state˺?” He replied, “Perhaps a day or part of a day.” Allah said, “No! You have remained here for a hundred years! Just look at your food and drink—they have not spoiled. ˹But now˺ look at ˹the remains of˺ your donkey! And ˹so˺ We have made you into a sign for humanity. And look at the bones ˹of the donkey˺, how We bring them together then clothe them with flesh!” When this was made clear to him, he declared, “˹Now˺ I know that Allah is Most Capable of everything.”

## Verse 2:275

> Those who consume interest will stand ˹on Judgment Day˺ like those driven to madness by Satan’s touch. That is because they say, “Trade is no different than interest.” But Allah has permitted trading and forbidden interest. Whoever refrains—after having received warning from their Lord—may keep their previous gains, and their case is left to Allah. As for those who persist, it is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

## Verse 2:282

> O believers! When you contract a loan for a fixed period of time, commit it to writing. Let the scribe maintain justice between the parties. The scribe should not refuse to write as Allah has taught them to write. They will write what the debtor dictates, bearing Allah in mind and not defrauding the debt. If the debtor is incompetent, weak, or unable to dictate, let their guardian dictate for them with justice. Call upon two of your men to witness. If two men cannot be found, then one man and two women of your choice will witness—so if one of the women forgets the other may remind her. The witnesses must not refuse when they are summoned. You must not be against writing ˹contracts˺ for a fixed period—whether the sum is small or great. This is more just ˹for you˺ in the sight of Allah, and more convenient to establish evidence and remove doubts. However, if you conduct an immediate transaction among yourselves, then there is no need for you to record it, but call upon witnesses when a deal is finalized. Let no harm come to the scribe or witnesses. If you do, then you have gravely exceeded ˹your limits˺. Be mindful of Allah, for Allah ˹is the One Who˺ teaches you. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

## Verse 2:286

> Allah does not require of any soul more than what it can afford. All good will be for its own benefit, and all evil will be to its own loss. ˹The believers pray,˺ “Our Lord! Do not punish us if we forget or make a mistake. Our Lord! Do not place a burden on us like the one you placed on those before us. Our Lord! Do not burden us with what we cannot bear. Pardon us, forgive us, and have mercy on us. You are our ˹only˺ Guardian. So grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”
